6.4     Upstream Triggered Spectrum Capture
6.4.1     Overview
Upstream spectrum analysis is one of the most important tools cable operators rely on to troubleshoot RF interference
in the return band. But given the return funnel8 effect in cable networks, locating ingress sources continues to be inference-
based, which is manual and tedious. Upstream triggered spectrum capture helps improve and automate this process.
The upstream triggered spectrum analysis measurement provides a wideband spectrum analyzer function by using the
CMTS burst receiver, which has knowledge of the scheduler. This is important because it can be triggered to examine
desired upstream transmissions as well as underlying noise/interference during a quiet period when no devices are
scheduled to transmit. It can also be triggered to capture on specific timestamps or time slots granted to a specific
MAC address.
In addition to having scheduler information available, the CMTS also has demodulators that run continuously on
active upstream channels. These demodulators can provide additional information for each of the channels to the
spectrum analyzer, such as RxMER and FEC statistics, that is useful for troubleshooting. These data can be used to
help automate and assist manual troubleshooting, such as by changing color indicators from red to green when
certain thresholds are exceeded.
In Figure 120 and Figure 121, the spectrum traces of the respective channels are colored green and red according to
their channel performance based on RxMER and FEC.




                Figure 120 - Upstream Spectrum Capture of Multiple Upstream SC-QAM Channels




8 Also referred to as noise funneling. Downstream signal transmission in a cable network is point to multipoint (headend or hub

site to multiple subscribers); upstream transmission is the reverse. Upstream interference such as noise or ingress can enter the
network at multiple locations and "funnel" back to the headend or hub, hence the reverse funnel effect.


01/14/21                                            CableLabs                                                                124
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 125 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                          CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114




                                 Figure 121 - Upstream Spectrum Capture of Quiet Time

In Figure 121, the first SC-QAM signal does not appear because the capture occurred precisely when there was no
scheduled upstream transmission. This allows noise power to be measured and the noise floor to be seen without the
presence of the SC-QAM signal on that frequency.

6.4.2      How to Implement
Vendor proprietary implementations and standardized CableLabs MIBs allow operators to take advantage of this
feature. Consult equipment manufacturers for the former. For the latter, see [CM-OSSIv3.1] and [CCAP-OSSIv3.1].

6.4.3      Conclusion and Suggestions for Future Work
Triggered spectrum capture is in use in CMTS line cards and in remote PHY equipment. Test equipment companies
have been integrating this capability into their products for some time, especially in distributed access architectures
where there is no RF returning to the headend or hub.
However, some CMTS manufacturers may not be following the specification to provide this feature in favor of
proprietary approaches. Therefore, significant effort or proprietary tools are required to benefit from upstream
spectrum capture methods. In general, there is some movement toward the methods outlined in the specifications.

6.5       Upstream Impulse Noise Statistics
6.5.1      Overview
This section describes the operation of the measurement of upstream impulse noise statistics.

6.5.2      Description of Measurement
The upstream impulse noise statistics measurement gathers data about burst and impulse noise occurring in a
selected narrow band as defined in [CCAP-OSSIv3.1]. To perform the measurement, a bandpass filter in the CMTS
is positioned in an unoccupied upstream band. A threshold is set, and energy exceeding the threshold triggers the
beginning of event measurement. Once an event has started, energy in the band is continuously monitored, and
energy falling below the threshold causes the measurement to stop and the statistics of the event to be recorded. The
CMTS may also allow the threshold to be set to zero, in which case only the average power in the band will be
measured. The measurement is timestamped using the 32-bit DOCSIS 3.0 timestamp (bits 9-40 of the 64-bit
extended timestamp, where bit 0 is the LSB), which provides a resolution of 98 ns and a range of 7 minutes. The
CMTS provides the capability to capture the following statistics in a selected upstream band up to 5.12 MHz wide:
      •    timestamp of event,



01/14/21                                        CableLabs                                                         125
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 126 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                              CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114


      •    duration of event, and
      •    average power of event.
The CMTS provides a time history buffer of these three measurements for up to 1,024 events.

6.5.3      Conclusion and Suggestions for Future Work
This measurement has not been field tested, other than proprietary implementations. Additional information will be
provided in a future version of this document.

6.6       Upstream FEC Statistics
6.6.1      Overview
This section describes the upstream FEC statistics.

6.6.2      Description of Measurement
The following text is taken from [PHYv3.1], Section 9.4.5, "Upstream FEC Statistics."
      Upstream FEC statistics provide monitoring upstream link quality via FEC and related statistics. Statistics are
      taken on codeword error events. An LDPC codeword that fails post-decoding syndrome check will be labeled
      "unreliable", but the data portion of the codeword may not contain bit errors; hence the "unreliable codeword"
      count will tend to be pessimistic. All codewords, whether full-length or shortened, are included in the measurements.
      The codeword (or frame) and error counters are long (64-bit) integers, so that rollover is not an issue.
The CMTS reports the following FEC statistics for any specified single upstream channel.
      •    Pre-FEC Error-Free Codewords: Number of codewords that passed pre-decoding syndrome check.
      •    Unreliable Codewords: Number of codewords that failed post-decoding syndrome check.
      •    Corrected Codewords: Number of codewords that failed pre-decoding syndrome check, but passed post-
           decoding syndrome check.
      •    MAC CRC failures: Number of frames that failed MAC CRC check.
      •    Total number of FEC codewords.
      •    Total number of MAC frames.
      •    Start and stop time of analysis period, or time that snapshot of counters was taken.
      •    SID corresponding to upstream user being measured.
The CMTS reports the following FEC summaries over a rolling 10 minute period for any single upstream channel:
      •    Total number of seconds.
      •    Number of errored seconds (seconds during which at least one unreliable codeword occurred).
      •    Count of codeword errors (unreliable codewords) in each 1-second interval (600 values over 10 minutes).
      •    Start and stop time of summary period.

6.6.3      Background Theory
At the CMTS, codewords are first assessed to determine if they are error free. If error free, they are counted as pre-
FEC error-free codewords. If they contain errors, the error correction of LDPC is applied to fix them. If a codeword
can be fixed, it is counted as a corrected codeword. If it cannot be fixed, it is counted as an unreliable codeword.
Independent of whether errors are fixed or not, codewords are stripped of their redundant bits and reassembled into
their original MAC frames. Once they are back in MAC frames, the CMTS uses the CRC to confirm if the MAC
frame is error free. If the MAC frame contains errors, it is counted as a MAC CRC failure. It is important to note
that, from the customer’s perspective, the remaining data will be perfect if the CMTS’s MAC frames are error free.




01/14/21                                          CableLabs                                                           126
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 127 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                            CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114


For each CM, the CMTS keeps counters for every type of codeword and MAC frame error listed above as well as
for both the total number of codewords the CMTS received and the total number of MAC frames the CMTS received.

6.6.4    Rolling FEC Summaries
With the 10-minute rolling record of errors in one-second intervals, the time characteristics of the errors may
identify the possible source of the interference. For example, if correctable errors occur in every sample interval,
then the possible source may be related to electrical arching from utility power. If uncorrectable errors occur in
several consecutive seconds, then the possible source maybe another data transmission. If long periods of errors are
separated by long periods of no errors, then the possible source may be a two-way radio transmission.
Examples are not yet available and will be provided in a future version of this document.

6.6.5    Conclusion and Suggestions for Future Work
Measurement has not been fielded. Additional information will be provided in a future version of this document.

6.7     Upstream Spectrum Capture at the CM
This section describes the use of full-band capture (FBC) in the upstream frequency range to isolate noise sources.

6.7.1    Overview
Traditional upstream capture techniques are obtained at the burst receiver in the CMTS or RPD, which is subject to
return noise funneling. This measurement is useful to manually troubleshoot noise, but it is particularly difficult to
locate noise sources. The difficulty is a side effect of HFC designs that use diplex filters and directional coupling to
facilitate upstream communication in lower frequency RF spectrum.
Cable modem upstream capture allows operators to sample the upstream RF spectrum at the cable modem. This
capability is important because the upstream RF spectrum is also subject to directional coupling such as port-to-port
isolation in the splitters and taps. The source can be quickly identified and located if the noise is entering at the
connection of the cable modem, ground block, drop, or tap connections.
Figure 122 shows a typical spectrum capture of the upstream without any discernable impairments in the return
band. In contrast, Figure 123 shows observable noise near and around the diplex cutoff frequency and interference
within the downstream SC-QAM, including FM radio. This result is a good indication that noise is likely present in
the return band, which the diplex filter is attenuating.




                             Figure 122 - CM Upstream Capture with Diplex Rejection




01/14/21                                        CableLabs                                                           127
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 128 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                            CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114




                       Figure 123 - CM Upstream Capture with Diplex Rejection and Noise



6.7.2    Description of Measurement
See Section 5.2 for descriptions of full-band capture.

6.7.3    Conclusion and Suggestions for Future Work
Although the upstream frequency range is not precluded from FBC in the DOCSIS suite of specifications, it has
limited usefulness in the presence of a diplex filter. There are cable modem reference designs that allow vendors to
implement the spectrum capture in a manner that bypasses the diplex filter without risk of leakage to the diplex filter
circuit. However, this capability is optional and may have limited value until wider adoption is achieved.

6.8     Upstream Histogram
6.8.1    Overview
This section describes the upstream histogram measurement. See Section 5.9 on downstream histograms for a
description of the measurement; the specifics are the same except as described in that section.

6.8.2    Example
As noted in Section 5.9, one-sided hard clipping starkly illustrates the effect of ideal laser clipping. In reality, the
result is more subtle because of softer, more gradual clipping or other effects such as bandlimiting. Figure 124
shows the histogram of a signal that has passed through an actual upstream cable plant with laser clipping. The
upper (rightmost) tail of the distribution, though not completely cut off, has been reduced noticeably below the
reference Gaussian distribution. The missing samples from the tail are evident in the fairly pronounced spike on the
right side of the distribution at around 1.25 standard deviations on the x axis. Although the effect is not as
pronounced as in an ideal simulation, this histogram could be analyzed by the appropriate algorithm to show a
strong indication of laser clipping, especially if a longer set of data is averaged.




01/14/21                                        CableLabs                                                           128
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 129 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                        CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114




                           Figure 124 - Histogram of Actual Upstream Clipping Event



6.8.3    Conclusion and Suggestions for Future Work
The upstream histogram measurement has not yet been developed in the field such that it adheres to specifications.
Additional information will be provided in a future version of this document.

6.9     Upstream Capture for Active and Quiet Probes
6.9.1    Overview
This section describes the operation of the upstream capture measurement for active and quiet probes.

6.9.2    Description of Measurement
The purpose of upstream capture is to measure upstream cable plant response and to view the underlying noise floor.
It does so by capturing at least one OFDMA symbol during a scheduled active or quiet probe (the quiet probe captures
the symbol period but no OFDMA symbol).
The PNM server selects an active CM to analyze by specifying its MAC address or requests a quiet probe measurement.
The CMTS selects a specified transmitting CM or quiet period when no CMs are transmitting for the capture. The
CMTS sets up the capture as described in [MULPIv3.1], selecting either an active SID corresponding to the specified
MAC address or the idle SID and defining an active or quiet probe. The active probe symbol for this capture
normally includes all non-excluded subcarriers across the upstream OFDMA channel and has pre-equalization off.
The quiet probe symbol normally has all subcarriers off; that is, there are no transmissions in the given upstream
OFDMA channel during the quiet probe time. The CMTS captures samples of a full OFDMA symbol including the
guard interval. The CMTS begins the capture with the first symbol of the specified probe. The sample rate is the
FFT sample rate (102.4 megasamples per second).
To ensure the input to the channel is known, the CMTS reports the list of excluded subcarriers, cyclic prefix length,
and transmit window roll-off period in order to fully define the transmitted waveform. The CMTS also reports the
index of the starting sample used by the receiver for its FFT, which helps specify the correct timing of the captured
samples. The CMTS reports the timestamp corresponding to the beginning of the probe, which provides information
as to when the measurement was made, for comparison between measurements and to correlate with plant conditions at
different times. When the P-MAPs for the OFDMA upstream being analyzed are being sent in an OFDM downstream,
the timestamp reported is the 64-bit extended timestamp. When there are OFDMA upstream channels but no OFDM
downstream channels, the reported timestamp is the 32-bit DOCSIS 3.0 timestamp (bits 9-40 of the extended
timestamp, where bit 0 is the LSB). For an active probe, the CMTS also reports the contents of the Probe Information
Element (P-IE) message, which gives detailed information describing that probe.
This is a time domain capture normally reported in the time domain to preserve the cyclic prefix.


01/14/21                                      CableLabs                                                         129
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 130 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                         CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114


6.9.3    Background Theory
An active probe provides the partial functionality of a network analyzer because the input is known and the output is
captured, permitting full characterization of the linear and nonlinear response of the upstream cable plant in the
OFDMA band being measured. A quiet probe provides an opportunity to view the underlying noise and ingress
while no traffic is being transmitted in the OFDMA band being measured.

6.9.4    Conclusion and Suggestions for Future Work
At the time of publication, the measurement has not been field tested. Work is ongoing to implement the
measurement and provide test results. This section will be updated as this measurement and its use cases develop.




01/14/21                                       CableLabs                                                        130
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 131 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                           CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114


7 EXAMPLES OF USE CASES
7.1     Use Cases Versus Measurements
Figure 125 was updated from [Currivan and Wolcott] and provides an example for a number of use cases. Future
versions of this document will contain additional details about use cases.




                           Figure 125 - Use Cases Addressable by PNM Measurements



7.2     LTE and Other Interference
Coaxial cables and other components used in the distribution and subscriber drop portions of a cable network provide a
shielded transmission medium that is independent of the over-the-air environment. Over-the-air radio frequencies are
allocated to various services by government agencies, but cable operators largely have the ability to use frequencies
within their closed networks as they see fit.
As long as the shielding integrity of a cable network is maintained, frequencies inside of the network’s cables and
components can be used for different purposes than what they are used for in the over-the-air environment. Should
the shielding integrity be compromised for any reason, over-the-air signals can leak into the cable network and
interfere with signals and services carried by that network, and signals inside the cable network can leak out into the
over-the-air environment and potentially cause interference.

7.2.1    Overview
Long-Term Evolution (LTE), or 4G, is a standard that describes high-speed wireless data telecommunications,
typically between user equipment (UE) such as smart phones, tablets, and other mobile devices and fixed tower and
similar sites. LTE operating frequencies range from 450 MHz to more than 3 GHz, depending on country, region,
and LTE band. Other frequencies in the over-the-air environment are used for services such as broadcast radio and
television, two-way radio, and aircraft navigation. Of particular interest to cable operators is RF signal transmission
on over-the-air frequencies that overlap frequencies used in cable networks.
Signal leakage, or egress, occurs when RF signals inside the coaxial cables and other cable network components can
leak out into the over-the-air environment and interfere with licensed services such as LTE. Ingress occurs when
over-the-air signals leak into a cable network and interfere with the cable network’s signals. (A variation of ingress




01/14/21                                        CableLabs                                                         131
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 132 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                            CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114


is called direct pickup interference.) In either scenario, the cable operator, not the provider of licensed over-the-air
service, is responsible for resolving the interference.
The ability to identify, troubleshoot, and locate potential sources of ingress and direct pickup (and perhaps signal
leakage) is a valuable part of PNM.

7.2.2    Signal Leakage
Cable network signal leakage is regulated by the respective federal government in many countries. If signal leakage
causes harmful interference to over-the-air signals, the cable operator can be subject to fines and/or temporary
suspension of the ability to use the affected frequencies until the harmful interference is eliminated. Figure 126
shows an example of signal leakage from a cable network, with SC-QAM signals overlapping LTE signals.




                    Figure 126 - Cable Network Signal Leakage in the 700 MHz LTE Spectrum,
                       Overlapping LTE Band 13 (Verizon) Tower-to-UE Downlink Signals


Although PNM tools cannot directly show signal leakage or provide a measurement of the leakage field strength, it
can detect the presence of ingress, as discussed in the next section. If a cable network has ingress problems, it likely
has signal leakage problems.

7.2.3    Ingress and Direct Pickup
Ingress is the unwanted entrance of over-the-air signals into a cable network because of degraded shielding
effectiveness of the network’s coaxial cables and other components. Direct pickup, similar to ingress, occurs when
the over-the-air signal(s) directly enters a susceptible set-top box, cable modem, television set, test instrument, or
other device directly without any cables or other external devices being physically connected. If the susceptible
device’s outer case or cover is inadequately shielded, then the internal wiring, printed circuit board traces, and/or
components can directly receive interfering over-the-air signals.
PNM tools can detect the presence of ingress interference and direct pickup interference in a few ways. One is
through full-band capture; the example in Figure 127 shows ingress from several sources, including LTE.




01/14/21                                         CableLabs                                                           132
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 133 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                       CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114




           Figure 127 - Full-Band Capture Spectrum from a Modem Showing Several Types of Ingress



A second way to use a PNM tool to detect the presence of ingress or direct pickup interference is to monitor the
RxMER on several frequencies. SC-QAM signals affected by ingress that might or might not be readily visible on a
spectrum display likely will have degraded RxMER compared to SC-QAM signals not affected by ingress or direct
pickup interference. For DOCSIS 3.1 downstream OFDM signals, a plot of RxMER per subcarrier can show the
effect that LTE or other ingress has on subcarriers that overlap the OFDM signal. See Figure 128. In this example,
ingress interference is visible where the RxMER drops abruptly, in the vicinity of 740 MHz, 760 MHz, and 870
MHz. It also shows evidence of possible narrowband interference at other frequencies.




    Figure 128 - OFDM RxMER-per-Subcarrier Plot Showing the Impact of LTE and Other Types of Ingress



Another method that can be used to detect the presence of ingress or other interference under digital signals,
described here for informational purposes, is based on deriving a spectrum from the signals’ error vectors. The
resulting error vector spectrum shows noise and ingress beneath the signal without having to turn off the signal.
Some cable industry test equipment vendors have incorporated variations of this technique in their products.
Figure 129 is an example screen capture from a DOCSIS 3.1 cable modem-equipped Viavi field meter showing the
noise floor (black trace) under an OFDM signal and the lower and upper adjacent SC-QAM signals (blue trace).




01/14/21                                      CableLabs                                                      133
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 134 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                             CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114




                        Figure 129 - "Ingress Under the Carrier" Display from a Field Meter



Detection and identification of ingress and direct pickup on a per-CPE basis can be incorporated in a PNM tool or
application to help cable operators locate where the ingress and/or direct pickup interference is entering the network.

7.2.4     How to Implement
Several ingress and egress detection methods for downstream and a few for upstream are described in other sections
of this document, including the sections on RxMER per subcarrier (Section 5.3) and spectrum analysis (Section 5.2).

7.2.5     Conclusion and Suggestions for Future Work
The ability to troubleshoot downstream ingress and similar interference is an important part of PNM. At least two of
the techniques described in this section—full-band capture and OFDM RxMER-per-subcarrier data—lend themselves
to being a useful component of a PNM tool. Downstream ingress and direct pickup interference can degrade cable
network signal quality. If ingress and/or direct pickup exists, it is likely that signal leakage is also present, which can
potentially cause interference to over-the-air services. The ability to identify and locate potential sources of ingress
can enable outside plant personnel to quickly fix problems.
Future work in this area might include incorporating error vector spectrum techniques in PNM tools to further support
identification and troubleshooting of ingress and direct pickup interference.

7.3     Fault Localization Based on Downstream RxMER
The PNM Best Practices guide for DOCSIS 3.0 systems [PNMP-3.0] outlined a fault localization process based on
correlating pre-equalization or full-band capture data from cable modems with their network topology to identify the
point along their shared path where a fault or anomaly indicator changes. Understanding where this change occurs
gives an indication as to the location or area of the cable plant that must be contributing to the fault. Based on field
observations subsequently validated in test lab environments, downstream RxMER-per-subcarrier data can be used
in a similar way to help locate faults.
The vicinity of the impairment can be identified by isolating end devices sharing a common path that show a ripple
in the RxMER per subcarrier caused by a micro-reflection or ingress interference resulting from a break in the
integrity of the cable plant. Comparing the reported RxMER per subcarrier of devices on the same shared path can
show a boundary between where a problem exists and where it does not. The fault, therefore, must be between the
last device showing the fault and the first device not showing the fault.

7.3.1     Fault Localization Examples
This section details basic examples of locating faults based on the reported downstream RxMER per subcarrier.
Additional details for these examples can be found in [Hranac and others].

7.3.1.1    Downstream RxMER Ripple
Figure 130 shows a location where all of the cable modems connected to the amplifier (A) show a significant ripple
in a plot of their downstream RxMER-per-subcarrier data. This ripple, however, is not seen on any cable modem
connected to any other output of the upstream amplifier (B). This analysis would therefore indicate that the cause of
the impairment is therefore located on the trunk cable (C) or connectors connecting amplifiers (A) and (B).



01/14/21                                         CableLabs                                                           134
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 135 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                        CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114




                  Figure 130 - Example of Downstream RxMER Data Showing Ripple Location
                                         Map data ©2019 Google; ©Akleza, Inc.

7.3.1.2    LTE Ingress
Figure 131 is an example of downstream RxMER-per-subcarrier data showing interference affecting a group of
cable modems all downstream of amplifier (A). Data from the cable modems connected on other outputs of the
upstream amplifier (B) do not show the same interference. The results of comparing the isolated group of impacted
cable modems and the group not showing the interference implies that the interference is related to a fault in the
trunk cable feeding amplifier (A), the connection into amplifier (A), or the amplifier itself.
A closer look at the impacted frequencies shows an overlap with the LTE Band 5 downlink transmission spectrum.
A survey of the area shows an antenna tower (C) close to amplifier (A), suggesting a possible integrity issue at or
around the amplifier is allowing the LTE signal to interfere with the DOCSIS 3.1 OFDM channel.

8 PNM DATA ISSUES
8.1    Data Collection Methods
In DOCSIS 3.0 systems, pre-equalization coefficient and full-band capture (FBC) data are obtained by using SNMP
to request information from CMs and CCAPs. The requested information is described in [PNMP-3.0], and the
SNMP MIB objects are defined in the [DOCS-IF3-MIB].
DOCSIS 3.1 technology introduced a new set of requirements on the CM and CMTS related to the collection and
reporting of PNM data (see [PHYv3.1], Section 9, "Proactive Network Maintenance"). DOCSIS 3.1 technology also
introduced a new Bulk-Data Transfer mechanism that uses TFTP to upload PNM data to a destination TFTP server.
Configuration of the PNM test execution, file storage, and TFTP destination uses SNMP as defined in [CM-
OSSIv3.1] and [CCAP-OSSIv3.1]. Figure 132 illustrates the steps and communication paths involved in collecting
PNM data from a CM or CCAP device.




                                 Figure 132 - Data Collection Sequence Diagram

The result of a successful PNM test and file upload is a PNM test result file that a PNM application can read and
analyze. By default, each PNM test result file name contains the PNM test, a device identifier such as the CM MAC
address, and a timestamp. The PNM test file is a binary data file containing a header and a data section as defined in
[CM-OSSIv3.1] and [CCAP-OSSIv3.1].


01/14/21                                       CableLabs                                                         137
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 138 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                        CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114


For example, a successful downstream RxMER test against a CM with the MAC address 480033DDF724 and
executed on Friday, March 22, 2019, 8:30:57 AM GMT would generate a PNM test result file with the name
PNMDsMer_480033DDF724_1553243457. This file is fully configurable. By parsing the data contained in the file,
a chart similar to that shown in Figure 133 can be plotted. This figure shows the RxMER per subcarrier across a
DOCSIS 3.1 OFDM channel.




                          Figure 133 - Example of Downstream RxMER per Subcarrier



8.1.1    Operational Considerations
The use of TFTP by the DOCSIS 3.1 PNM Bulk-File Transfer mechanism may require some network configuration
changes if any firewalls are between the CM or CCAP and the destination TFTP server. TFTP is a UDP-based
protocol that uses ephemeral ports to transfer data. Figure 134 shows the upload sequence in which Ports X and Y
are ephemeral port numbers.




                                        Figure 134 - TFTP Upload Diagram



For the TFTP upload process to complete, any network firewalls between the CM or CCAP and the TFTP server
must be configured to allow UDP traffic from the ephemeral port range between the CM or CCAP and TFTP server
to pass. Though it may be possible to use source and destination addresses to configure an explicit rule for CCAP
devices, it is not practical for CM subnets. As such, rules allowing UDP Port 69 and all UDP traffic in the ephemeral
port range destined for the TFTP server are required to support the PNM Bulk-File Transfer mechanism.



01/14/21                                      CableLabs                                                        138
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 139 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                           CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114


8.1.2    Security and Reliability Considerations
TFTP and SNMP are currently not specified to be encrypted. However, one can add application layer security if
desired. For example, HTTPS proxy can be added in front of data collectors like the Common Collection
Framework (CCF), which allows encrypted posts and gets.
UDP, which is low priority, is the assumed transport mechanism. As such, a capacity limit is reached if there is too
much traffic, and the UDP packets can be dropped, causing a loss of PNM information. Further, there is a general
assumption that if a test fails or if information is corrupted in the transfer process, then a retry of the test is
warranted. The application layer (in this case the PNM application) is assumed to manage the information reliability.
In addition, CMs can be limited in their processing of PNM information. If a PNM request takes 5 minutes to
complete and is only initiated when requested, then the CM will not likely respond to other requests until the first is
completed. Consider this when requesting data from CMs.

8.1.3    Deployment Considerations
The network needs to have a TFTP server to receive and hold the TFTP files that are responses to the data requests.
Also, firewall rules on the network must allow the TFTP packets to travel from the CM to the server and to any
applications that need the data.
Similarly, SNMP requires firewall rules to be configured to allow the traffic in both directions.
Virtual machines (VMs) are commonly used to deploy PNM data collectors such as CCF. The VM should have
enough cores and be configured properly for the deployment size. It is common to have one PNM data collector
instance for each CMTS core, but different models are possible and should be considered based on expected use.

8.1.4    IPDR Bulk Data collection
Although the current DOCSIS specifications that cover PNM require SNMP and TFTP mechanisms for data
delivery, some cases require upstream data from the CMTS be obtained via IPDR.
Spectrum data are also specified for SNMP by DOCSIS 3.0 technology, but billing data is in IPDR only. IPDR may
require a command-line interface to the CMTS.

8.2     Measurement Calibration
Discussion of this topic in a future version of this document is being considered. For now, it is acknowledged that
calibration is an important factor when comparing measurements from different sources.




01/14/21                                        CableLabs                                                         139
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 140 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                                  CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114


9 PNM PRACTICES
9.1       Measures of Access Network Health
This section provides a starting point for measuring access network health, which can be used as a performance
measure for network operations, and potentially as a measure for selecting maintenance to prioritize.
There are two related concepts considered in this section: the quality of downstream (and upstream) plant, and the
quality of downstream (and upstream) service with respect to the plant condition. This section first focuses on
downstream measures, but also mentions briefly how to address this question for the upstream too.
General guidelines in summary are as follows.
      •    Quality of downstream plant – Start with RxMER per subcarrier, translate to bit loading, and use RxMER
           stats to assess when spectral efficiency is a concern. This is currently accessed on the CM via the
           CmDsOfdmRxMer MIB. But to access the actual bit loading, the table CmDsOfdmModProfile is used. A
           new measurement, it provides modulation profile by subcarrier. CM firmware is being updated as of this
           publication date, so this capability may become more common soon. There should be considerations for
           input signal level and FEC stats as well. Too low or high of a signal level can indicate problems, and very
           high correctable codewords or any uncorrectable codewords can indicate vulnerability to smaller
           impairments. To determine outliers, the values need to be compared to expectations, which should come
           from design guidelines, and may be specific for each operator.
      •    Quality of downstream service with respect to downstream plant – Use RxMER margin for the given
           profile for the CM, then sum the margin over all subcarriers. This is currently managed with the
           CmDsOfdmMerMargin MIB. CmDsOfdmModProfile data may be of use for this measurement too.
           Considerations should be given to the service provided to the customer too. More specifically, a low
           latency service may not tolerate retries as much as typical internet service.
      •    Averages over any period of time consisting of at least 10 samples, using statistical average or
           exponentially weighted moving average, for a stable measure. This would be managed outside the CM by
           the cable operator retaining the measures above over time and then assessing the data over that period to
           determine patterns and impacts over the period. Operators typically sample PNM statistics each hour,
           though some sample less frequently still. This is mostly a factor of the maturity and capability of the data
           collection systems available to the operators.
      •    Averages over any population or random sample from a population of CMs using statistical average to get
           estimates for the plant section. Samples should include at least 50% of the population for populations under
           100, and at least the maximum of 20% or 50 CMs for larger populations (assumed, should be tested, should
           be sufficient). This item would be managed outside the CM by the cable operator retaining the measures
           above over time and then assessing the data over that period to determine patterns and impacts over the
           period. These measures should generally be collected over a given node.

9.1.1      Quality of downstream plant (for each OFDM signal)

9.1.1.1     RxMER per subcarrier approach
1.    Retrieve RxMER per subcarrier data (say once per hour or every two hours) using the CmDsOfdmRxMer MIB.
2.    Calculate the bit loading appropriate for each subcarrier, applying any operating margin desired9. Use the
      CmDsOfdmRequiredQamMer object to determine the RxMER target values required in each subcarrier, then
      determine the appropriate bit loading by comparing the RxMER values to the target values, per subcarrier. If
      docsPnmCmDsOfdmMerMarTable is not available, the default values from the specification are provided
      below. (Note that, when available, this can be done (or confirmed) using CmDsOfdmModProfile table.)
3.    Translate into maximum symbol size if and as desired.
4.    Use any of these three as the measure of downstream plant quality.

9 See section I.3 of this technical report which discusses FEC and RxMER per subcarrier test results and guidelines for operators

for more information on this subject.


01/14/21                                            CableLabs                                                               140
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 141 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                                 CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114


The bit loading recommendations from the current OSSI specification [CM-OSSIv3.1], and the similar table of
CMTS Minimum CNR performance in AWGN channel from , are shown in the tables below, though operators may
choose to adjust these recommendations to their own needs.

     ReqMerQam256             UnsignedInt        R/W                                      quarterDb         108
     ReqMerQam512             UnsignedInt        R/W                                      quarterDb         122
     ReqMerQam1024            UnsignedInt        R/W                                      quarterDb         136
     ReqMerQam2048            UnsignedInt        R/W                                      quarterDb         148
     ReqMerQam4096            UnsignedInt        R/W                                      quarterDb         164

     ReqMerQam16384           UnsignedInt        R/W                                      quarterDb         208
     Table Note: the table above reports integer values of ¼ dB values, so translation to dB values may be needed in some
     cases. For example, ReqMerQAM128 reports a value of 96 in ¼ dB, which is equivalent to 96/4 =24 dB.

                Constellation               CNR1,2 (dB)                   Set Point                      Offset
                                                                       (dBmV/6.4 MHz)

            Note 1. CNR is defined here as the ratio of average signal power in occupied bandwidth to the average
              noise power in the occupied bandwidth given by the noise power spectral density integrated over the
              same occupied bandwidth.
            Note 2. Channel CNR is adjusted to the required level by measuring the source inband noise including
              phase noise component and adding the required delta noise from an external AWGN generator.
            Note 3. The channel CNR requirements are for OFDMA channels with non-boosted pilots. For operation with
              boosted pilots, which is optional at the CMTS, the CNR requirements are increased by
              a) 1 dB for channels with 50 kHz subcarrier spacing, and
              b) 0.5 dB for channels with 25 kHz subcarrier spacing. For example, the CNR requirement for QPSK with
              boosted pilots is 12.0 dB with 50 kHz subcarrier spacing and 11.5 dB with 25 kHz subcarrier spacing.



9.1.1.2     RxMER stats approach
1.   Poll RxMER stats at a suitable rate using CmDsOfdmRxMer MIB.
2.   Identify any outlying values of 2nd percentile or 5th percentile statistics, and poll RxMER per subcarrier, then
     use the RxMER per subcarrier approach above to find their quality measure.



01/14/21                                           CableLabs                                                               141
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 142 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                                 CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114


3.   For those RxMER stats with nth percentile values not far from the mean, apply a uniform bit loading to estimate
     the bit loading possible on the whole channel.
4.   Translate into a maximum symbol size as desired.
5.   Use any of the three results as measures of downstream plant quality, recognizing that those based on RxMER
     stats are approximations.
The nodes with outlier 2nd percentile or 5th percentile values can be quickly identified for closer examination. The
standard deviation can also provide further insight into where to focus maintenance.

9.1.2     Quality of downstream service with respect to downstream plant
This section provides only a view of current practices with a view of general service health. Much more work should
be done for consideration of specific services and use cases, including impact on latency, real time use cases, and
other specific use case considerations. The general guidance in this section serves as a base and starting point for
more development by operators who choose to differentiate. But what is provided her is informative toward
maintenance decisions, which is an ultimate goal of making service health actionable.
Certainly, timeouts and sub-optimal profile assignment are key indicators of service health problems. With the ease
of access to PNM measurements like profile, RxMER per subcarrier, and FEC stats, more can be done after timeout
issues are discovered.

9.1.2.1    RxMER margin and profiles for downstream health
For downstream service quality, the profile in use is important. The profile in use can be compared to the service
offered, and judgement about the suitability of the profile for providing the level of service may be rendered.
1.   Select the desired profile, preferably the current or most likely used profile for the given CM.
2.   Poll RxMER margin at a suitable interval of time using CmDsOfdmMerMargin MIB, providing the desired
     threshold offset value (ThreshldOffset).
3.   Use either the number of subcarriers below threshold as the measure of quality, or repeat with a more suitable
     profile if NumSubcarriersBelowThrshld is greater than 0.
A possible replacement for network health indication for correctable error when using OFDM channels is the
number of iterations it takes to correct all of the errored bits in an LDPC codeword. However, this is not available
today. Our studies comparing FEC statistics to RxMER, which is used to assign downstream profiles, reveals the
complex relationship between these measurements. In Section 9.3 of this document, those test results and guidelines
for the use of FEC stats for maintenance decisions are provided.

9.1.3     Consideration of FEC for Upstream health
RxMER statistics are not easily obtainable in the upstream today. But when available, they can be used in a similar
way as described for the downstream. FEC statistics are not generally available in the upstream.
A possible replacement for network health indication for correctable error when using OFDM or OFDMA channels
is the number of iterations it takes to correct all of the errored bits in an LDPC codeword.
Low Density Parity Coding (LDPC) is used for the inner Forward Error Correction (FEC) of both DOCSIS OFDM
and OFDMA channels. LDPC differs from traditional Reed-Solomon (RS) Encoding in that it uses much larger code
words spread across all of the channel.
Traditionally FEC errors, both corrected and uncorrected, have been used as an indication of network health. With
the introduction of DOCSIS 3.1 OFDMA and OFDMA channels, a more aggressive (powerful) FEC scheme is used,
specifically LDPC. With LDPC coding it is normal to have corrected errors on a healthy channel. It can even be
argued that if there are no FEC errors that the channel is being underutilized. Seeing uncorrectable errors is still an
issue, as it indicates packet errors and loss.10



10 Further CRC check at Ethernet MAC level is required to determine which packets, if any, are errored.




01/14/21                                           CableLabs                                                        142
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 143 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                             CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114


LDPC decoders are iterative, if a codeword is not successfully decoded on first pass, a second pass is performed.
The number of decoding passes continues to increase until either the codeword is successfully decode, or the
codeword is declared uncorrectable. The number of passes is implementation dependent.
Currently the only way to observe the number of iterations is in the trailer of UEPI packets sent from RPDs. These
trailers include a count of the average number of iterations for each LDPC codeword size. This is defined in Table 8
of the [R-UEPI]specification. It is possible to capture UEPI packets and monitor these counts on a per channel basis.
The UEPI interface is used for Remote PHY connections to RPDs.

9.1.4    About Averages
For a single CM sampled over a time period, make the samples as evenly spaced as possible over the time period,
but also account for any known cyclical issues such as temperature, time of day, weather, etc. For example, for a
single day quality estimate, sample at least 10 times over the day, which may be every two hours for convenience,
for a total of 12 samples. For a monthly sample, collect over random parts of the day for every other day of the
month.
For a group of CMs, an average of results taken over a population or sample from a population for approximately
the same time interval is best. For a point in time, sample the CMs as closely as possible to that time. For an interval
of time, collect samples over the time to cover it well; anything larger than a few hours will require multiple samples
from each CM (see above).
Averaging can be done over time using either the statistical average (sample sum over sample size) or an
exponentially weighted moving average to weigh current values higher than past values, providing a measure that
might better account for random fluctuations yet be a current representation of quality.

9.1.5    Sampling Frequency for Intermittent Problems
Intermittent problems are well known to be difficult to capture. If it is assumed that intermittent issues are
essentially random, or that there is no knowledge about when they generally occur, then using observations of
variations through periodic sampling much catch them.
If intermittent impairments arrive randomly at E events per day with an expected duration of D seconds per event,
then the proportion of the day that an intermittent event is happening is
Pevent = E (events/day) * D (seconds/ event) / (24*60*60 (seconds/day))
Likewise, to capture these events with a probability Pcapture, then it takes n samples of the data to capture it as
         Pcapture = 1-(1-Pevent)n
Solving for n, results are
         n = ln(1-Pcapture)/ln(1-Pevent)
where ln is the natural log.
To find the impairment with the given probability Pcapture over say m days, then it is sampled at a rate of S = n/m per
day. Also, finding the minutes between samples is
         I = 24*60/S.
The table below shows a few example results.
                                            Table 10 - Calculate Sample Frequency

                E                 D               Pcapture         m                S                  I
            Imp. Freq.         Imp. Dur.        Prob. Catch     Scan Dur.       Scan Freq.       Scan interval
            events/day           sec               0<x<1          day           sample/day       min./sample
                 300                0.010           0.50             30            665.410            2.164
                 100                0.010           0.50             30            1996.252           0.721

                 300                0.100           0.50             30             66.531            21.644




01/14/21                                            CableLabs                                                        143
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 144 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                            CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114


                E               D             Pcapture            m                S                  I
            Imp. Freq.       Imp. Dur.      Prob. Catch        Scan Dur.       Scan Freq.       Scan interval
            events/day         sec             0<x<1             day           sample/day       min./sample
                 300            0.010            0.90               30            2210.443           0.651
                 300            0.010            0.50               10            1996.229           0.721

                 100            0.100            0.50               30            199.615            7.214
                 100            0.010            0.90               30            6631.407           0.217
                 100            0.010            0.50               10            5988.757           0.240

                 100            0.100            0.90               30            663.106            2.172
                 100            0.100            0.90               10            1989.318           0.724


Note that some of these scenarios requires frequent sampling, which is not possible for some measurements such as
RxMER per subcarrier, or full spectrum capture.

9.1.6    Stability
If these health indicators are not stable over time, then that lack of stability can also be an indication of poor network
or service health or both. There are several methods for adding variability in measurements to a health indicator.
•   A measurement range over time is a good indication of variability or lack of stability and can be weighted into
    the measurement itself.
•   Using weighted averages of the measurements, so that poor values are weighted more heavily or for a longer
    period of time, can also make intermittent poor values weight nearly as much or as much as consistently poor
    values, as desired.
    • For more heavily weighted values, consider a quadradic loss function, such as the square of the difference
        from the value to the ideal value.
    • For weighing values over time, consider an exponentially weighted moving average or window average
        with a weighted function of the values in the average.

9.1.7    Impairments
Consideration of impairments and their severity is worthwhile as well. See Section 9.2 on the network maintenance
decision process and the discussion of impairment severity for a severity-based approach worthy of consideration.
Note also that the paper “Profile Management Informed Proactive Network Maintenance” from SCTE Expo 2020
[PMI PNM] provides a comparison of several methods of network and service health for selecting maintenance.
Also, there are recent SCTE papers published which apply machine learning techniques to detect anomalies
(impairments) in RxMER per subcarrier or spectrum data: [Zhu, Sundaresan, Rupe], [Ferreira, Harb, et al].

9.1.8    Conclusions
In the downstream, RxMER per subcarrier measurements are a great indicator of network and service health. FEC
statistics can be a contributor to health indication too, particularly in the upstream; but FEC errors in the downstream
indicate a health problem beyond what profiles are addressing, and RxMER statistics can indicate.
When these health measures are used for identifying proactive work, this section provides high level starting
positions for that work. But there are additional features to consider as outlined in Section 9.2 ,“Network
Maintenance Decision Process”, below.
Other measures such as T3 and T4 timeouts may be indicators as well but are not proactive in nature; T3 and T4
timeouts may indicate noticeable service degradation which should be addressed through maintenance. This section
focuses on the problem of prioritizing proactive maintenance work, so focus is just on those indicators that inform
that problem. FEC statistics and RxMER in combination work very well as a starting point for this. But as found in
[PMI PNM], profiles can have a significant impact on the selection of proactive work, though clearly profiles are not
the best indicator of network or service health when RxMER and FEC statistics are available.



01/14/21                                        CableLabs                                                           144
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 145 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                            CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114


9.2       Network Maintenance Decision Process
The network maintenance tools often referred to as PNM tools are useful for all types of network maintenance,
proactive and otherwise. It is not helpful to differentiate between proactive and reactive maintenance, especially
when making use of maintenance tools. Many of the methods outlined in this report work for all types of
maintenance use cases.
There are many identifiable elements that contribute to the decision to do maintenance, be that proactive or reactive.
      •    Problem identification – that it exists, and location – if a customer calls in the problem, then you have an
           easy place to start. But that can be misleading too. If the problem is broader, the problem may not be near
           that customer, so additional work may be needed to isolate the problem, and certainly to identify it. If using
           network information to identify a problem, then you are actually on your way to identifying the issue faster
           than a customer call.
      •    Significance of impact – if a customer calls in the problem, you have a good view to at least part of the
           problem. Others may be impacted, and additional impact may not be felt immediately at or before the
           customer calls. If using network information to gage significance, you may be able to quantify the impact
           in more uniform terms.
      •    Sufficiency of information to address the problem – when a customer calls, you know you must address the
           service issue. But if using network data to determine a network issue that may not yet be a service issue,
           then an important part of the decision process is whether or not enough information is available to make the
           problem addressable.
      •    Cost effectiveness to address the problem – sometimes opportunities, like a mandatory reactive repair, will
           lower the cost of a proactive repair, making it an imperative. When considering the degradation and further
           damage that is possible, some proactive tools can allow operators to get ahead of costly repairs.
      •    Resources available to address the problem – when ahead of the problem, operators can plan and schedule
           maintenance when resources are available and less expensive.
      •    Success probable – in learning how to associate network data with service and network conditions, data
           can be collected and tracked, increasing the likelihood of successful resolution. This will inform planning
           and decision making for maintenance.
      •    Priority for operations – With all the information outlined above, operators are equipped to prioritize work
           and optimize operations spend.
The distinction between proactive and reactive maintenance is subtle, and not so cleanly separate.
Operators who are fully occupied with reactive maintenance can still get ahead of the work to be proactive
defendants of service. By embracing some of the newer network operations tools which are based on network data
that are available through network elements already, they can troubleshoot more efficiently and effectively. Once
operations becomes more efficient, and resources are freed up, then those resources can be more focused on the
network data that foretells of network problems. Eventually, operations falls down the slippery slope of proactive
network maintenance simply by being efficient.
By using the proactive tools, sometimes in reactive ways, operators will slip into doing more proactive work simply
because it is more efficient and better for service to do so. There really is no hurdle to get over; simply mix in
proactive when and where it makes sense to do, and you will be over any perceived hurdle. By using PNM tools
effectively, for reactive problems too, operators find that reactive repairs can be streamlined, freeing resources for
proactive work. Mixing proactive with reactive increases the opportunity density, reducing wasted truck rolling time
and increasing overall operations efficiency.
Keep in mind that there are network problems that DOCSIS will work around, and thus hide if you aren’t using
proactive tools to look. These don’t necessarily impact service but can contribute to smaller issues causing service
impact that otherwise wouldn’t and may indicate network damage that must be addressed. Some network damage
puts service at risk, and some will get worse and more expensive if left alone. Changes in performance
measurements can indicate which problems are new versus which are getting worse versus which are stable.
Note also that the paper [PMI PNM] from SCTE Expo 2020 provides a comparison of several methods of network
and service health for selecting maintenance. From that paper, the following conclusions can be used:




01/14/21                                         CableLabs                                                          145
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 146 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                            CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114


      “Profile management has a clear influence on the impact to service that results from PNM. Therefore, some
      consideration of profiles is important when selecting proactive work to do. It is advisable that operators at least
      look at the impact on profiles when determining whether to conduct PNM, and it is likely a factor in prioritizing
      proactive work.
      Anomaly-driven PNM, finding and eliminating anomalies, appears to be the most popular approach that
      operators take for their PNM; but modem-driven PNM is an option to consider for some, as the data that most
      informs PNM comes from the CMs. Likewise, profile-driven PNM is an option to consider as well. But
      ultimately, each of these factors needs to be considered in some way in deciding which PNM work to take on.
      Though this paper does not compare all options, an examination of the options presented here does suggest that
      impairment-driven identification of maintenance opportunities is a very good approach, that problem severity
      can be measured by impact on CMs, and that it is necessary and complimentary to PNM decision making to
      consider the impact on profiles when determining the capacity impact on the network for selecting maintenance.
      Said differently—find anomalies, investigate their impact on customers’ service, estimate the impact due to
      profiles and on network impact expected from the work, and use these pieces of information to prioritize and
      select PNM opportunities. It may at times be best to let profiles drive PNM maintenance decisions, in fact.
      Seeing that some network repairs can have negative impact on profiles, it suggests more work on profiles is
      warranted too. It may be simple enough to set a rule that says, if all CMs improve, then the recalculation of
      profiles can only result in a net benefit, or a benefit to each CM, or another appropriate rule, before
      consideration of replacing profiles. In other words, do not harm first. Alternately, it may be useful to develop
      algorithms that set profiles to be robust against these changes. Likewise, it may be worth investigating different
      objectives when setting profiles altogether.”
A methodology and framework for building and testing various maintenance decision processes is available from
CableLabs and is explained in [Rupe, Zhu]

9.3     FEC, MER and Decoding in the Downstream
Cable operators have for many years tracked pre-FEC and post-FEC bit errors in legacy SC-QAM based digital
video and DOCSIS networks. Those legacy SC-QAM signals, based on ITU-T J.83, use concatenated coding with
outer-code Reed-Solomon forward error correction. The onset of pre-FEC bit errors for the Reed-Solomon decoder
has long been used by many operators to determine that performance degradation is starting to occur, and that the
cause of the pre-FEC errors should be identified and fixed before post-FEC bit errors occur. Post-FEC bit errors
result in packet loss.
DOCSIS 3.1 introduced a more powerful FEC to the cable industry, known as low density parity check (LDPC).
Because of the way LDPC works, it is not possible to rely upon the presence of correctable codeword errors to
indicate the onset of potential problems. Indeed, one can have 100% correctable codeword errors and expect
generally reliable modem operation, with no real indication of how close to the digital cliff one is. What to do?
One proposed possible solution is to track RxMER per subcarrier (relative to the RxMER threshold for the
modulation order in use) along with correctable and uncorrectable codeword errors. The InGeNeOS Working Group
commissioned CableLabs to do this testing, and the test results are in Appendix I.
Some plots in the test results of that appendix show FEC decoder input, which is the sum of the correctable
codewords and the uncorrectable codewords as a percentage of total codewords. These are the codewords that failed
pre-decoder syndrome check. So, one minus the FEC decoder input is the percent of error free codewords after
slicing and prior to decoding. It is easiest to tell which data in the figures are from FEC decoder input as they stay
flat (and nearly 100%) at below-threshold MER values while the underlying uncorrectable codeword percent
increases as MER decreases from above-threshold to below-threshold.
Total codewords is the sum of the number of error-free, correctable, and uncorrectable codewords. FEC decoder
input is the sum of the correctable codewords and the uncorrectable codewords. All are reported as a percentage of
total codewords.
Note on codeword error terminology: The DOCSIS 3.1 downstream FEC includes LDPC and BCH, while the
upstream uses only LDPC. In the downstream, the BCH can be thought of as being analogous to a broom and
dustpan, sweeping up residual errors, and providing the ability to get correctable and uncorrectable codeword error



01/14/21                                         CableLabs                                                          146
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 147 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                                  CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114


counts. Among the available downstream FEC statistics are: uncorrectable codewords (the number of codewords
that failed BCH decoding); correctable codewords (the number of codewords that failed pre-decoding LDPC
syndrome check and passed BCH decoding); and the total number of FEC codewords.
In the upstream, because there is only LDPC, one cannot get the same FEC statistics that are available in the
downstream. Among the available upstream FEC statistics are: pre-FEC error-free codewords (the number of
codewords that passed pre-decoding syndrome check); unreliable codewords (the number of codewords that failed
post-decoding syndrome check); corrected codewords (the number of codewords that failed pre-decoding syndrome
check, but passed post-decoding syndrome check); and the total number of FEC codewords.
Replicated with permission, from R. Prodan, L. Montreuil, A Kliger, B. Shen, “Demystifying the DOCSIS 3.1
PHY,” SCTE Cable-Tec EXPO, September 22-25, 2014, [Prodan, et al], see the following two unlabeled figures and
explanatory text which supports these results.
“DOCSIS 3.1 employs several forward error correction codes for various purposes. This section will discuss both
the downstream and upstream forward error correction codes for protecting the payload user data. Several relevant
characteristics for these FEC codes are as follows:
Downstream Code:
     •    DVB-C211 (16200,14400) inner LDPC + (14400,14232) outer BCH12
     •    Overall Rate 0.8785
Upstream Codes:
     •    (16200,14400) Quasi-Cyclic LDPC Rate 0.8889
     •    (5940,5040) Quasi-Cyclic LDPC Rate 0.8485
     •    (1120,840) Quasi-Cyclic LDPC Rate 0.75
The bit error ratio performance as a function of SNR for the downstream FEC code for all square, non-square, and
mixed modulation modes from 64-QAM to 4096-QAM is shown in the figure below. The 10-8 bit error ratio SNR
threshold for each modulation order is tabulated from this graph. The spectral efficiency is given by the code rate
times the number of bits per QAM subcarrier for a given modulation order. The resultant spectral efficiency
(bits/s/Hz) vs. SNR is shown in figure 16.13 The Shannon capacity limit is also shown in this figure to evaluate the
relative proximity of the performance of the downstream FEC code. The figure shows this FEC code to be about 3
dB from the Shannon limit or about one bit/s/Hz below the Shannon limit.”

as part of the quoted material.

From [Sundaresen, Zhu, et al], Section 3, see results that align with these test results where there is overlap, and the
work in our new paper confirms and extends the work in this paper. While our work tested a wider range of
conditions and CMs, this referenced paper covered the case of profile switchover. If profiles are allowed to switch in
a given implementation, the adjustments may continue to keep service stable (avoid uncorrectable codeword errors),
though at a cost of data rate. But if profiles are not allowed to be switched, then the uncorrectable codeword errors
may impact service.
Outer BCH coding leads to definitive determination of errored codewords, unlike LDPC which only indicates error
free assurance. LDPC alone leaves open whether or not codewords not absolutely error-free are errored.
LDPC is used for the inner FEC of both DOCSIS OFDM and OFDMA channels. LDPC differs from traditional
Reed-Solomon (RS) Encoding in that it uses much larger code words spread across all of the channel.




01/14/21                                           CableLabs                                                               149
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 150 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                            CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114


Traditionally FEC errors, both corrected and uncorrected, have been used as an indication of network health. With
the introduction of DOCSIS 3.1 OFDMA and OFDMA channels, a more aggressive FEC scheme is used,
specifically LDPC. With LDPC coding it is normal to have corrected errors on a healthy channel. It can even be
argued that if there are no FEC errors that the channel is being underutilized. Seeing uncorrectable errors is still an
issue, as it indicates packet loss.
LDPC decoders are iterative: if a codeword is not successfully decoded on first pass, a second pass is performed.
The number of decoding passes continues to increase until either the codeword is successfully decode, or the
codeword is declared uncorrectable. The number of passes is implementation dependent.




01/14/21                                        CableLabs                                                           150
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 151 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                                  CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114


Appendix I               Error Correction Margin Solution Analysis
I.1           Introduction
Cable operators have for many years tracked pre-FEC and post-FEC bit errors in legacy SC-QAM based digital
video and DOCSIS networks. Those legacy SC-QAM signals, based on ITU-T J.83, use concatenated coding with
outer-code Reed-Solomon forward error correction. The onset of pre-FEC bit errors for the Reed-Solomon decoder
has long been used by many operators to determine that performance degradation is starting to occur, and that the
cause of the pre-FEC errors should be identified and fixed before post-FEC bit errors occur. Post-FEC bit errors
result in packet loss.
DOCSIS 3.1 introduced a more powerful FEC to the cable industry, known as low density parity check (LDPC).
Because of the way LDPC works, it is not possible to rely upon the presence of correctable codeword errors to
indicate the onset of potential problems. Indeed, one can have 100% correctable codeword errors and expect
generally reliable modem operation, with no real indication of how close to the digital cliff one is. What to do?
One proposed possible solution is to track RxMER per subcarrier (relative to the RxMER threshold for the
modulation order in use) along with correctable and uncorrectable codeword errors.
There is interest in DOCSIS 3.1 OFDM RxMER per subcarrier values at which correctable and uncorrectable
codeword errors begin to appear. This is with and without profile changes. There is also interest in the triggering of
a profile change as an indicator. Finally, operators want to confirm the effectiveness of a nominal 6 dB of operating
headroom above the RxMER threshold for the modulation order in use, and identify risks with operation at lower
headroom values.
Several controlled tests were conducted at CableLabs, with the test procedures and results summarized in this
document.

I.2           Error Correction Margin Test Plan

I.2.1       Test Set A
Test set up 1 is similar to section 3.3 of [Sundaresen, Zhu, et al], an SCTE PMA paper. An Agilent/Keysight
N5182A MXG Vector Signal Generator (VSG) with AWGN option as an AWGN source was used.
A test plant was set up with at least two manufacturers of each chip set (total four CM manufacturers) and at least
two CMs of each copy for a total of at least eight CMs, connected to a splitter setup. That splitter was fed by a
nearby OFDM source, to avoid the need to use an external amplifier.
First, the RxMER responses for these CMs and test set up to get consistent levels (flat input) were calibrated .
Then configured test conditions were configured so the CMs get equal received power levels. There were four sets
of tests: one set each at +10 dBmV, 0 dBmV, -6 dBmV, and -10 dBmV RF input to the modems.
For this first set of tests, all (static) profiles were fixed to 4096-QAM.14 In most cases, where not indicated, a 192
MHz wide channel was used.
The remaining procedures were as follows. Obtain a baseline reported RxMER per subcarrier from the DOCSIS 3.1
modems (data collected via TFTP from the CmDsOfdmRxMer object) when the CNR is in the high 40s (ideally
there should be no correctable codeword errors reported) at the desired RF input level(s). Using a Keysight UXA,
measure OFDM RxMER per subcarrier too. 15



14 It was desired to put together a test set-up that has high enough CNR to be above the point where correctable codeword errors

show up. For instance, if testing 4096-QAM, that would require a CNR in the high 40s.
Keep in mind that RxMER per subcarrier values much above the mid 40s (as reported by a cable modem) may be suspect. For
such high RxMER values, some significant portion of the measured noise may be contributed by the CM or UXA, and as such
may not be a precise indication of the input to the measuring device.
15 In figures and text showing results, sometimes the Keysight UXA measurements are described more generally as VSA for

vector signal analyzer.


01/14/21                                            CableLabs                                                               151
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 152 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                              CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114


Introduce noise level uniformly on all frequencies first. Start injecting wideband AWGN (enough to cover the entire
OFDM signal spectrum), increasing its amplitude such that the carrier-to-noise ratio (CNR) at the modems is able to
be reduced in 1 dB steps during the tests (smaller increments as the limits were reached). At each step record
RxMER per subcarrier, and FEC stats (uncorrected, correctable, and uncorrectable codewords). Uncorrected
codewords are codewords that were determined to contain no errors even prior to decoding. Look for the onset of
correctable codeword errors. Increase the noise level (decrease the CNR) until uncorrectable codeword errors reach
50%, or if not possible then at least until correctable codeword errors decline for at least three increases are seen16.
As CNR is reduced in 1 dB steps, correctable codeword errors will eventually show up. Note the value at each 1 dB
drop in CNR. Repeat until the onset of uncorrectable codeword errors. Keep dropping CNR until the OFDM signal
no longer passes any data at all.
Test set up 2: repeat with an attenuator value to achieve 0 dBmV RF input.
Test set up 3: repeat with an attenuator value to achieve -6 dBmV RF input.
Test set up 4: repeat with an attenuator value to achieve -10 dBmV RF input.

I.2.2       Test Set B
Repeat test set A but with controlled (simulated) LTE ingress instead of AWGN.

I.2.3       Test Set C
Repeat test with AWGN configured to produce a CNR near (about 3 dB above) the RxMER (SNR) threshold (as
reported in specifications and repeated later in this section). Then add LTE ingress, and increase the strength of the
LTE signal until failure.

syndrome check. So, one minus the FEC decoder input is the percent of error free codewords after slicing and prior
to decoding. It is easiest to tell which data in the figures are from FEC decoder input as they stay flat (and nearly

codeword errors start to show up. When within about 6 dB of the RxMER threshold for a given modulation order, without ingress
or other severe impairment, the correctable codeword errors are near or at 100% all the way down to the RxMER threshold.

input is the sum of the correctable codewords and the uncorrectable codewords. All are reported as a percentage of
total codewords.

I.3          Relating FEC to RxMER in OFDM for avoiding uncorrectable codeword errors

I.3.1      AWGN flat noise only
In the following plots, the MER(dB) values are derived from ratios of signal power and error power at the OFDM
demodulator’s slicer for each subcarrier, for both the CM and UXA measurements. The MER for these curves
correspond to the average RxMER (averaging the “dB” values of each subcarrier RxMER value in “dB”); because
the test condition is intended as a flat channel with flat noise (AWGN) there is some, but minimal, variation in the
RxMER per subcarriers. Further, some plots show FEC decoder input, which is the sum of the correctable
codewords and the uncorrectable codewords as a percentage of all codewords.




                             Figure 137 - CM and VSA Input Signal Power -10 dBmV
