01/14/21                              CableLabs                                           155
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 156 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                        CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114




                             Figure 140 - CM and VSA Input Signal Power +10 dBmV


Refer to Figure 137 through Figure 140. Note that the figures show FEC decoder input, which is the sum total of all
correctable and uncorrectable codewords. Also recall the test configuration that the UXA (which is our vector signal
analyzer (VSA)) is connected at the same time in the same setup with the CMs, so both measurements are taken at
the same time, but resulting in different dB values.
Also note that the UXA measures RxMER using data subcarriers instead of pilots as the CM uses.
•   CM and UXA measurements do not agree on the average RxMER values. CMs seem to perform with no
    uncorrectable codeword errors (100% correct codewords) down to a level just below 36 dB RxMER on their
    own measure, whereas the UXA reports a higher RxMER value corresponding to the FEC threshold.
•   CMs differ somewhat in the RxMER level at which they take on errors (correctable and uncorrectable), though
    in this set of tested CMs the difference is not very large.
•   There is at least a 6 dB range between where a) the CM sends nearly 100% of the data through the decoders and
    b) the CM has no uncorrectable codeword errors after decoding, resulting in nearly 100% correctable codeword
    errors.
•   As uncorrectable codeword errors appear, increasing the AWGN slightly to reduce the CNR by less than 0.5
    dB, the CM loses connection (sync) with the CMTS.
•   These results suggest that,
       As long as the (average) RxMER is above 36 dB (for 4096-QAM) the CM will perform fine under flat
        AWGN.



01/14/21                                      CableLabs                                                        156
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 157 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                            CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114


         If a given plant has low variability in its noise level, operators could expect service to remain reliable much
          closer to the 36 dB limit, so could set an operating headroom threshold at a lower RxMER value than if a
          plant has high variability with noise. In other words, while more reliable operation is expected when
          RxMER is 6 dB or more above the threshold, one could operate with less headroom if the plant is stable
          and free of ingress.
•   Issues to be studied include
         What if noise is focused over a subset of frequencies (such as with ingress).
         Are there differences in these results for different modulation orders, or OFDM channel sizes?
         Does LTE have a different impact than random noise over the same frequencies.
To address these issues to be studied, bin noise (such as narrow band ingress) to the test will be introduced.
As power level seems not to matter with these tests, only testing at 0 dBmV signal power level in subsequent tests
will be done.

correctable codeword data points (instead of FEC decoder input) and uncorrectable codeword data points, forming
what is called “whale plots.” Most of the rest of our results will be showing whale plots, but either can be created
from the same data results.

I.3.2        LTE Ingress Results with Correctable and Uncorrectable Codeword Errors (Whale Plots)
This subsection reports the results from our testing with simulated LTE ingress, or notch noise. First, testing with an
8 MHz-wide noise inserted into a 96 MHz-wide channel, with 94 MHz modulated spectrum (only band edge
excluding subcarriers) demonstrates that CMs have trouble staying synchronized and connected to the CMTS.


First run:
Channel width: 96 MHz
Modulated spectrum: 94 MHz
Profile modulation: 4096-QAM
PLC frequency: 333 MHz

 Figure 142 - Whale Plots of 0 dBmV Power Level, 4k-QAM, 96 MHz-wide Channel, 8 MHz-wide LTE Ingress,
                     with Increasing LTE level to Reduce the RxMER in the LTE Band.
                             MER (dB) in these Plots is a Notch MER (dB) Value


The MER (x-axis) values in Figure 142 through Figure 144 are the average RxMER in the spectrum where the LTE
signal is present. Figure 142 shows the 96 MHz-wide channel is overtaken by the 8 MHz-wide LTE ingress as the
LTE signal power is increased, which decreases the MER value, and results in CMs losing sync at the lower MER
values for the notch. Therefore, to avoid losing data from the CMs losing sync, the following tests are done with a
wider channel to allow the CMs to remain connected.
Note also that there is a 13 dB difference between the notch MER for approximate threshold in all cases in
comparing the CM MER and VSA MER curves. In all cases the approximate threshold MER for the notch is about
13 dB (plus or minus a dB) higher for the “VSA MER” curve set.

Figure 142 through Figure 144 indicate the average MER value over the simulated LTE ingress channel, but

channel to allow resiliency mechanisms to keep the CMs connected and working. All of these three figures report
the average MER (dB) over the LTE channel. As expected, this wide of an LTE ingress does not disrupt the CM
entirely until uncorrectable codewords begin to show, then there is no room for variability in the MER values.
To keep this new test comparable to the previous, subsequent plots of the same data were provided but for the
average RxMER value over the full 192 MHz channel instead of the RxMER value in the simulated noise band, to
better allow comparisons.

with Increasing LTE Level to Reduce the RxMER in the LTE Band, this Time Reporting Average RxMER over
                                          the Whole Channel




Figure 146 - Whale Plots of 0 dBmV Power Level, 256-QAM, 192 MHz-wide Channel, 8 MHz-wide LTE Ingress,
with Increasing LTE Level to Reduce the RxMER in the LTE Band, this Time Reporting Average RxMER over
                                          the Whole Channel


If profiles are fixed, new or intermittent ingress or spurious impairments will cause problems sooner than if profiles
can adjust to compensate for the presence of impairments, or profiles are set to be robust against known
(intermittent) impairments.
Seeing a drop in a profile can indicate that a problem has occurred, and there may be a reason to investigate.
But lacking a drop in the profile, and looking at average RxMER, there is only about 2 dB of room between taking
on correctable codeword errors and having a significant number or even full uncorrectable codeword errors (making
the CM lose connection and the data not showing in the plot). This observation suggests that any time a CM is
experiencing correctable codeword errors it may be vulnerable to LTE or other ingress or channel noise. If an
operator is not able to manage a tight plant, it may be important to manage profiles conservatively, or keep a large
margin in RxMER.

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 163 -->

Figure 148 below shows the same results as above (Figure 147), but plotting the average RxMER values instead of

01/14/21                                     CableLabs                                                     165
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 166 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                             CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114


Figure (above) shows different values of noise floor, and increasing the LTE level to lower RxMER values in the LTE
frequency range; this time the results are plotted against average RxMER over the signal, not just the LTE.
It doesn’t seem to matter how much noise floor there is; the LTE level dominates.
At 100% correctable codeword errors you are vulnerable (about 2 dB loss overall and uncorrectable codeword errors
could begin). Therefore, again, when considering issues like in-channel ingress, to the extent it’s possible, you don’t
want to have CMs operating with correctable codeword errors.
However, it may be helpful to start looking at correctable codewords, then examining the RxMER per subcarrier data
for “notch” noise, and measuring the RxMER values in the notch, to determine how close the CM is to taking on
uncorrectable codeword errors.

I.4           Conclusions
It is normal and expected behavior to see 100% correctable codeword errors in the downstream when the RxMER per
subcarrier is within about 6 dB of the RxMER threshold for the modulation order in use. Good engineering practice
suggests maintaining at least 6 dB of operating headroom above the RxMER threshold for reliable operation.
Following this guidance, the minimum recommended operating RxMER for 4096-QAM is about 41~42 dB, which is
about 6 dB above the approximately 35~36 dB threshold for that modulation order (see the graphs in the Addendums
section). As long as the cable network remains stable and impairment-free – and there are no uncorrectable codeword
errors – the correctable codeword errors can be ignored.
Some cable operators choose a smaller amount of headroom, as low as 2 dB or 3 dB, above the RxMER threshold.
While this may be satisfactory in a stable and impairment-free plant, the presence of impairments can increase the risk
of degraded performance.
Observations from the lab testing suggest that, while CMs can indeed operate reliably within about 6 dB of the RxMER
threshold, the risk of problems increases when in-channel spurious noise and interference are present. Degraded
performance may be more likely to occur, such that the issue(s) should be addressed proactively. Even a small bit of
ingress could severely impact service. At the very least, if a CM is experiencing any amount of correctable codeword
errors, and there is evidence of ingress or similar in-channel interference, even though small, it should be addressed.
In fact, these results suggest that any ingress should be addressed in a cost-effective way.
Further, it is best to have an OFDM signal wide enough that any expected amount of LTE or other interference does
not cause CMs to lose connection. For instance, when the bandwidth of LTE ingress exceeds approximately 5% of the
bandwidth of the OFDM signal, CM performance is more likely to be impacted.
In all cases, some form of profile management is recommended, whether built-in to the CMTS/CCAP or part of a third-
party or standalone tool. With profiles that can tailor to noise issues in some bands, further resiliency can be had, and
an imperfect profile can also indicate an issue in the plant that should be addressed before disrupting service too. The
combination of profile management and sufficient operating headroom will ensure the most reliable overall OFDM
performance.




01/14/21                                         CableLabs                                                          166
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 167 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                            CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114


Appendix II            Future Technology
II.1          Technology Evolution
The cable industry has benefitted from seemingly nonstop technology evolution since it began in 1948. Since 2008,
that evolution has paralleled developments in PNM. Though this document focuses on PNM for DOCSIS 3.1 systems,
PNM will no doubt play a role in future network architectures and technology. The following sections discuss
recently introduced and possible future technologies.

II.1.1     Technology Milestones
To help understand where the cable technology might be headed, it is helpful to understand where it has been.

II.1.1.1        From Vacuum Tubes to Solid State
The first community antenna television (CATV) systems were built in the late 1940s and served primarily rural
communities where over-the-air television reception was difficult or impossible. Vacuum tube amplifiers were used
for signal distribution (the amplifiers were installed in large metal boxes on utility poles). Early cable networks
carried 1 to 3 television channels; later, up to 12 channels were provided to subscribers, with a maximum operating
bandwidth of 220 MHz. The first solid state, i.e., transistorized, amplifiers were "drop-in" replacements for some of
the vacuum tube amplifiers. Some solid state amplifier designs of the 1960s more closely resemble their modern
counterparts. Today's outside plant actives support operation on frequencies as high as 1.2 GHz and can carry the
equivalent of hundreds of video channels, telephony, and high-speed data services.

II.1.1.2        Satellites
In 1975 a small regional company called Home Box Office became the first program service to use geostationary
satellite technology to deliver television programs to cable systems. Other program services soon followed, and the
use of satellites revolutionized the industry, providing justification for cable operators to obtain franchises (licenses)
and build networks in larger metropolitan areas. Geostationary satellites are still used today by the cable industry.

II.1.1.3        Network Architectures
Until the late 1980s, cable networks used an all-coax tree-and-branch architecture. Tree-and-branch was a cost-
effective "broadcast" architecture. On the downside, cascaded active devices degraded signal quality, and cascaded
components (actives, passives, power supplies, connectors, etc.) reduced reliability.
The year 1988 marked the beginning of what is now called hybrid fiber-coax (HFC) architectures. Multichannel
amplitude modulation-based optical fiber links augmented coaxial cable to distribute services to cable subscribers.
HFC architectures segmented cable networks into smaller serving areas, initially with several thousand homes
passed per node. The use of fiber reduced cascaded devices, improved quality and reliability, and reduced operating
costs. These benefits carry over today as cable operators push fiber deeper into the network, further reducing use of
cascaded devices and components and improving overall reliability and availability. What is called a node+0
architecture (no actives after the node) can have as few as 50 homes passed per node.

II.1.1.4        From One-Way to Two-Way
Most early cable systems were one-way, which means that signals were transmitted through the cable network in one
direction only: from cable company to subscriber. When the cable industry embarked on major network upgrades in
the 1990s, two-way operation became widespread. In a two-way cable network, signals are transmitted to and from
the subscriber simultaneously but in different frequency ranges. Figure 149 shows an example of a subsplit band plan,
in which downstream signals are transmitted to the subscriber from 54 MHz to 1.2 GHz and upstream signals are
transmitted from the subscriber in the range from 5 MHz to 42 MHz. Other band plans use different frequency splits,
with the upstream spectrum operating to as high as 204 MHz and the downstream spectrum starting at 258 MHz.




01/14/21                                         CableLabs                                                           167
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 168 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                         CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114




             Figure 149 - Example of Subsplit Band Plan Used in North America and Other Regions



II.1.1.5       High-Speed Data over Cable
Deployments of high-speed data service over cable networks in the early- to mid-1990s used proprietary technology,
which meant there was no interoperability among different manufacturers’ products. If a cable operator purchased
modems from a given vendor, the operator also had to use that vendor’s headend equipment.
In 1996, the cable industry became involved in an effort to develop cable modem standards, primarily to promote
interoperability. Similar work had started two years earlier under the auspices of the Institute of Electrical and
Electronics Engineers, Inc. (IEEE) 802.14 Cable TV Media Access Control and Physical Protocol Working Group.
In January 1996, four cable operators—Cox Communications, Comcast Cable Communications, Tele-Communications,
Inc. (TCI), and Time Warner Cable—formed a coalition known as Multimedia Cable Network System Partners Limited
(MCNS). MCNS issued a request for proposal (RFP) for the research and publication of interface specifications for
high-speed data services using cable modems. An interoperability specifications consortium comprising Continental
Cablevision, Rogers Cablesystems, and Cable Television Laboratories (CableLabs) joined MCNS, and the group
announced its preliminary Data-Over-Cable Service Interface Specifications (DOCSIS) in December 1996. The final
MCNS specification, issued in March 1997, supported nominal downstream data rates from 27 to 38 Mbps and
upstream data rates from 320 kbps to 10 Mbps.
The International Telecommunications Union (ITU) officially approved the DOCSIS 1.0 specification as an international
standard in March 1998, and the first DOCSIS 1.0 CMTS and CMs were qualified and certified respectively by
CableLabs in early 1999. Given the success of DOCSIS technology, the IEEE’s 802.14 efforts were formally
disbanded at the end of 1999.
The DOCSIS 1.0 specification was followed by DOCSIS 1.1, 2.0, and 3.0 specifications. The DOCSIS 3.1 suite of
specifications was introduced in October 2013. DOCSIS 3.1 technology supports scalability to 10+ Gbps in the
downstream and 1+ Gbps in the upstream. It is being used by cable operators to provide gigabit-class data service.

II.1.1.6       Distributed Access Architectures

II.1.1.6.1          Remote PHY
Remote PHY (R-PHY) is a network architecture in which the media access control (MAC) layer is separated from
the physical layer (PHY). The MAC layer remains in the CMTS/CCAP core (in the headend or hub) and the PHY
electronics are located in a shelf or node. A 10-Gb Ethernet digital fiber link connects the core and a remote PHY
device (RPD), which is installed in an R-PHY shelf or R-PHY node. Among the benefits of R-PHY are the ability to
overcome limitations of CMTS/CCAP core port scalability as fiber is pushed deeper into the network, as well as
improved RF performance because of the switch from analog fiber links to digital fiber links.
Several vendors have R-PHY and related products available today, and interoperability testing has been underway at
CableLabs and in the labs of various cable operators. Interoperability testing ensures that RPDs and cores (physical
or cloud native) from different vendors work together. Some operators are in (or are planning) lab and field trials;
others are already deploying R-PHY technology in fiber-deep (e.g., node+0) and business-as-usual (e.g., node+m)
applications and in new builds, rebuilds, and node splits.

II.1.1.6.2          Flexible MAC Architecture
Originally called Remote MAC-PHY, in which both the MAC and PHY electronics are located in a node, the
concept has been changed somewhat to Flexible MAC Architecture (FMA). As its name suggests, FMA supports
flexibility in the location of the MAC layer. The target architecture includes a remote MACPHY device (RMD),
installed in a shelf or node; a remote MAC core (RMC), installed in a shelf or node; a physical MAC core (pCore),




01/14/21                                       CableLabs                                                         168
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 169 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                           CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114


located in a separate chassis; and a virtual MAC core (vCore), located in a server. Benefits are similar to R-PHY
architectures and include a claimed reduction in latency between the MAC and PHY.

II.2         What's Next

II.2.1     Full Duplex DOCSIS Technology
Full duplex (FDX) DOCSIS technology is an extension of DOCSIS 3.1 technology and supports symmetrical multi-
gigabit service offerings (e.g., 5 to 10 Gbps). The latter is achieved in part by simultaneous transmission of downstream
and upstream signals on the same frequencies (see Figure 150). A key part of FDX DOCSIS technology is expansion
of the upstream spectrum to as high as 684 MHz (or more) versus 42 MHz or 85 MHz today, yielding the potential
for 10 to 50 times more upstream data throughput. FDX DOCSIS technology is optimized for fiber deep (node+0)
with R-PHY nodes. The FDX-capable node includes echo cancellation to support simultaneous use of the same
frequency ranges by downstream and upstream signals. The reader is directed to the most recent version of the
DOCSIS 4.0 Physical Layer Specification [PHYv4.0] for more information.




           Figure 150 - Frequency Spectrum Usage in FDX DOCSIS Systems (Bottom) Compared to
                         Traditional Subsplit, Midsplit, or High-Split Band Plans (Top)



II.2.2     FDX Amplifier
In order to use FDX DOCSIS technology in architectures beyond node+0, some vendors have proposed the concept
of FDX amplifier technology, in which the amplifier also is equipped with echo cancellation. In principle, an FDX
amplifier would support deployment of FDX DOCSIS technology in HFC architectures with amplifier cascades
after the node, for example, node+2 (or more).

II.2.3     Extended Spectrum DOCSIS Technology
The [PHYv3.1] specification defines a downstream upper frequency limit of 1,218 MHz (optional to 1,794 MHz).
The concept of extended spectrum DOCSIS technology embraces operation at frequencies above 1.2 GHz, to as
high as 3 GHz, to support downstream data speeds of up to 25 Gbps, and upstream speeds of 5+ Gbps. Figure 151
shows examples of extended spectrum DOCSIS technology with and without FDX.




   Figure 151 - Extended Spectrum DOCSIS Frequency Usage With and Without FDX DOCSIS Technology




01/14/21                                        CableLabs                                                          169
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 170 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                            CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114


II.2.4     10G Initiative
The 10G initiative was announced in early 2019 at the Consumer Electronics Show by NCTA, CableLabs, and Cable
Europe. The initiative’s speed target for cable networks is symmetrical 10 Gbps and will offer lower latency and better
security and reliability. Multiple technologies are part of 10G: DOCSIS 3.1 and 4.0 technology, full duplex, coherent
optics, wireless, and more. For additional information on the 10G initiative, see https://www.cablelabs.com/10g.

II.2.5     Remote PHY Tap
Moving the remote PHY device to the tap and feeding the tap with digital fiber could potentially support a dedicated
RF spectrum to each subscriber. Taking advantage of the relatively short length of the typical subscriber drop and
the very high transverse-electromagnetic (TEM) mode cutoff frequency for drop-size coaxial cable, it may be
possible for an R-PHY tap to support operating frequency ranges as high as 8 GHz to 12 GHz, with data speeds of
100 Gbps in the downstream and 25+ Gbps in the upstream.

II.2.6     Wireless PNM
Wi-Fi is the most common way customers access cable services, but the overwhelming majority of customer support
calls are Wi-Fi related. Whether measured in direct support costs or customer churn, it is an expensive problem to have,
making the development of Wi-Fi PNM critical. In June 2019, the Wi-Fi Alliance introduced Wi-Fi CERTIFIED Data
Elements™, which provides a standardized data model targeting Wi-Fi performance and gives operators reliable,
actionable information. Predictive analytics makes proactive troubleshooting possible and allows remote corrective
action to take place before the customer notices an issue. For example, if neighboring Wi-Fi is using overlapping
channels, then intelligent analysis of Wi-Fi PNM data can reveal the problem, and instructions for a channel change
can be sent to the CPE. Not only does this help the direct customer, but the net impact is improved service for all—
especially critical in multiple dwelling units. As cable operators work to improve customer experience and reduce
operations costs, Wi-Fi PNM will be a critical pillar in any solution architecture.

II.2.7     Optical PNM
Beyond coherent optics and its unique relationship to cable services, optical networking is becoming a more prominent
part of how cable services are provided. Optical technology can be more reliable, but it is often more complex and can
still fail. In fact, as optical elements are used nearer to the core of networks, they often carry more traffic, sometimes
for more customers, than a single coax cable. This setup creates a larger risk group, which often makes the impact of
failures more significant. For example, analog dense wavelength division multiplexing (DWDM) and direct-detection
or coherent optics make general monitoring of communication performance on optical links more important. But
unlike with DOCSIS systems, operators do not have the same resiliency to problems in some optical networks, so
they have fewer chances to be proactive. Still, optical impairments can be monitored, and monitoring often indicates
a problem before service is impacted.
Optical networks often have redundancy in the core for protection and restoration, allowing services to be carried with
higher reliability and availability than on a single connection. Monitoring these connections affords proactive repair
in that though a failure of a single link means higher latency or greater risk, service may be maintained. Thus, repairs
on these networks are proactive from a service perspective even though they are reactive from a network perspective.
Even simplex networks, often closer to the edge, have opportunities for proactive maintenance. Optical networking
relies on methods like DOCSIS technology to provide bit- and packet-level protection, as well as error correction
methods. These measures work like DOCSIS technology to create a gap between a network impairment and a service
failure so that operations can receive an indication of a problem before service is impacted. Bit errors can indicate a
number of problems, such as a laser or driver circuit in a small form factor pluggable transceiver about to fail or an
optical cable stressed by a physical issue. And like DOCSIS systems, optical networks have time domain reflectometry
technology that allows impairments to be located and isolated to aid in repair.
For these reasons and use cases, plus many more not described here, the cable industry will rely on PNM solutions for
the optical portions of the network. Optical PNM is under development and will continue to progress in the future.

II.2.8     Coherent Optics
The evolution of HFC networks towards fiber-deep strategy and distributed access architectures (DAA) will lead the
digital optical portion in cable networks to increase. Digital optics, especially the introduction of coherent optics into


01/14/21                                        CableLabs                                                           170
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 171 -->
Primer for PNM Best Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                          CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V02-210114


access networks, enables rich intelligence through digital signal processing (DSP) that takes place within optical
transceivers. Many of the same PNM processes that the industry leveraged in the RF domain can be applied to the
optical domain through advanced trend analysis of characteristic performance parameters. Coherent optical systems
provide a better way to support fault forecasting, detection, diagnosis, and localization on the optical terminals and
link systems. Additionally, they can also provide signal health information for both traditional analog and intensity-
modulated access networks in coexisting optical transmission systems.

II.2.9     Fiber to the Home (FTTH)
Fiber to the home (FTTH) is not new technology, but it has not been deployed widely by the cable industry (at least not
in brownfield applications) because of the cost. FTTH has been deployed in some new-build (greenfield) applications
over the years, using radio frequency over glass (RFoG) or passive optical network (PON) technology. It is expected
that FTTH will become more economical over time, and variations of it could be deployed on a more widespread basis.

II.2.10    Conclusion and Suggestions for Future Work
It is anticipated that PNM can and will be developed for future cable technologies. At its heart, PNM is about service
resiliency. A resilient service will continue to deliver with quality despite network impairments and failures. As long
as the industry can identify failure modes for network technologies and create resiliency mechanisms for networks,
it can identify how to measure and manage these resiliency methods and use them to create opportunities for PNM.
